The patient's severe sleep disturbance and feeling 'extremely jumpy' for three weeks suggest an anxiety- or trauma-related condition. PTSD and Acute Stress Disorder are considered because hyperarousal and insomnia can follow a traumatic event, with the 3-week duration fitting Acute Stress Disorder if a trauma occurred. Generalized Anxiety Disorder remains possible because persistent anxiety with insomnia and restlessness can present this way, though the time course is short. Adjustment Disorder is also possible if symptoms began after a clear stressor and do not meet criteria for a more specific disorder. It's really just the nightmares and being constantly on edge—I jump at every little sound, touch, or sudden movement, and it takes forever for my racing heart to calm down.\n\nDoctor: Have these symptoms lasted for more than one month since the event, or are they still within the first month?\n\nPatient: They are still within the first month. It has been exactly three weeks since the event happened and these symptoms started.\n\nDoctor: Have you also been having distressing memories or flashbacks of the event, or is it mainly the nightmares and feeling on edge?\n\nPatient: No, I don't really get daytime flashbacks or distressing memories of the event. It is definitely the nightmares and feeling extremely jumpy, like being easily startled by sudden noises, unexpected touch, or movements, and then taking forever to calm down.\n\nDoctor: Since the event, have you felt very irritable, had trouble concentrating, or been having a lot of anxiety or panic-like symptoms during the day as well?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really noticed feeling particularly irritable or having trouble concentrating. I also haven't experienced daytime anxiety or panic; my struggles are really just limited to the nightmares and being so jumpy.\n\nDoctor: Have these symptoms been causing major problems in your daily life, like work, school, or relationships, or do they feel more like a strong reaction to the event that is starting to settle?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure how to measure that, and I haven't thought deeply about how it's affecting my work or relationships yet. I just know that the sleep issues and being so easily startled have been consistently bothersome and haven't started to settle down at all over these past three weeks.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding places, people, conversations, or activities that remind you of the event?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really noticed myself avoiding any people, places, or conversations since it happened. I haven't been trying to steer clear of anything like that."

Adjustment Disorder remains possible because symptoms followed a stressor and are bothersome, but the prominent trauma-linked nightmares and hyperarousal make Acute Stress Disorder more fitting.   {
    "role": "system",

Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.
